We need to prove strong normalization for CBV STLC with locally nameless representation. Let me think about the approach.

The language: locally nameless with fvar (free variables as atoms) and bvar (bound variables by index). The typing rules use cofinite quantification (T_Abs with L : list atom) and open t1 (tm_fvar x).

CBV semantics: ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t) and value v. Note the step relation ST_AppAbs: tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open t v. ST_App1 requires locally_closed t2. ST_App2 requires value v1.

Strong normalization for CBV STLC. Reducibility candidates with biorthogonality or with a reducibility interpretation.

Standard approach: Define logical relation on terms indexed by type:
- For Ty_Bool: t is reducible iff SN t (and maybe closed? no — but for open terms with fvars we need substitution interpretation).
- For Ty_Arrow: t reducible iff SN t and for all u reducible at T1, (tm_app t u) reducible at T2. Actually in CBV, we need to handle neutral terms and expansions. Since the step relation is deterministic (actually it is a deterministic CBV small-step relation), we can define:
  - R_Bool = SN (strongly normalizing).
  - R_{T1->T2} = { t | SN t /\ forall u, R_T1 u -> R_T2 (tm_app t u) }.

But for the fundamental theorem with open terms? Actually the typing judgment is about open terms with fvars in context. We prove a fundamental theorem over contexts using a substitution interpretation: for a context Gamma, an environment sigma from atoms to terms such that for each x : T in Gamma, R_T (sigma x). Then for any term t with Gamma |-- t \in T (where t may contain fvars), the "closing substitution" of t by sigma is in R_T.

But locally nameless: T_Abs rule gives body closed with fvar x under context extension. We need to reason about `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` and substitution.

Alternative approach: avoid general substitution interpretation over all forms by defining reducibility on terms with fvars directly as "closed under all substitutions" — a semantic type interpretation defined on open terms:

Define:
- `R T t` for arbitrary tm t (possibly with fvars):
  - R Bool t := SN t (for any t? Or only locally closed? maybe SN t for all t; but fvars are SN since they don't step... fvar x has no step rules, so it's SN).
  - R (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u). Hmm but wait for CBV, values of function type are abstractions; a term of function type that is SN but not a value cannot take a step as function in app... Actually application with non-value function steps via ST_App1 until it becomes a value. So reducibility for arrow should say: t SN and for all u with R T1 u, tm_app t u is R T2. This is the standard Girard-Tait reducibility for CBV (with SN included in arrow). Actually typically for CBV you define:
    - RC of base type: SN terms.
    - RC of arrow: t is SN and for all u in RC(T1), app t u in RC(T2).
  This works, since app t u with t not a value but SN still reduces by ST_App1 (requires locally_closed u! Hmm ST_App1 has a side condition locally_closed t2).

Hmm, the semantics require locally_closed t2 for ST_App1. Since our terms in the relation should be locally closed? Actually the supplied definitions might ensure via typability that the term is locally closed (all bvars are bound), since the typing rules only produce terms built from fvars and well-scoped bvars. But has_type doesn't enforce lc explicitly; however terms constructed from the syntax of typing derivations: in T_Abs, the conclusion is tm_abs T1 t1, where t1 may have dangling bvars? The premise types `open t1 (fvar x)`, which replaces bvar 0 with fvar x; deeper bvars remain. Could t1 have bvar 1 free? open t1 (fvar x) would have bvar 0 dangling (since open_rec k with k=0 shifts? Wait open_rec 0 u t: matches tm_bvar i, if i = 0 then u else tm_bvar i. It doesn't decrement indices. In locally nameless with this "open" definition, opening does not decrement. Hmm, actually this looks like a strange open: for standard locally nameless, `open_rec k u t` replaces bvar k with u and does NOT shift down. But then under a binder it goes to S k, so it replaces the variable k only. The lc_at definition: lc_at k t means all bvars i < k are allowed? Wait lc_bvar: i < k -> lc_at k (tm_bvar i). Hmm, so lc_at k allows bvars less than k, meaning lc_at 0 allows none. But wait, in standard LN, `lc_at k` means bvars < k may occur, i.e. k bound variables in scope. And open_rec k replaces bvar k (the next free index).

But typical LN opening decrements indices above k? No—in the "cofinite" style from Software Foundations, `open_rec k u t` replaces `tm_bvar k` with u, and there's no decrement; that's why they call it "opening" with named variables: under abstractions they increment k. Actually SF's STLC_adt uses:
```
Fixpoint open_rec (k: nat) (u: tm) (t: tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | bvar i => if k =? i then u else bvar i
  ...
```
Yes, same. And lc_at k means all bvars < k. Wait but in SF, `lc_at k t` means all variables with index < k are bound? Let me recall: In SF's LocallyNameless chapter:
```
Inductive lc_at : nat -> tm -> Prop :=
| lc_var : forall k x, lc_at k (var x)
| lc_bvar : forall k i, i < k -> lc_at k (bvar i)
| lc_abs : forall k t, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k (abs t)
...
```
Yes, that's exactly it: lc_at k t: all free bvars in t are < k. OK.

Note: value v_abs requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t) — i.e., lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t) = lc_at 1 t. So values are closed abstractions. ST_App1 requires locally_closed t2. ST_AppAbs requires value v! In CBV, app (abs) u where u is not a value steps via ST_App2 (since abs is a value) to app (abs) u' where u --> u'. Standard CBV: applications reduce the argument to a value before β. So in the candidate definition, we need a more careful argument: For t value of arrow type (abstraction), app t u reducible for reducible u. Standard Tait for CBV defines reducibility for values separately, or uses a "strongly normalizing + closed under expansion" style.

Let's think carefully. Common approach: define two mutually inductive predicates `red T t` and `redv T v` for values:
- redv Bool v := v = true \/ v = false (or value v with...?).
- redv (T1->T2) v := forall u, red T1 u -> red T2 (app v u)  (plus SN v maybe)
- red T t := SN t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> red T t') and if t is a value then redv T t? Hmm.

A common formulation (from Harper's PFPL / Girard): 
- t ∈ R_T for base: SN t.
- For arrow: t ∈ R_T iff SN t and for all u ∈ R_T1, app t u ∈ R_T2.
But then showing app (abs ...) u ∈ R_T2 for u ∈ R_T1 requires: SN(app (abs) u) — since u is SN, by induction on the SN derivation of u: if u is a value, app steps to open body u and body is reducible via the abstraction's premise; if u steps to u', app steps to app (abs) u' and by IH that's reducible; plus closure under steps. That's doable but requires proving things by induction on SN u with a lemma about app of a "function-reducible" term.

Let me think about the standard "reducibility candidates" for CBV in locally nameless where values matter. There's the notion of "Kripke-style" or "logical relation with expansion": define R_T as the set of terms t such that SN t and for every t' that is a "value-like" expansion... Hmm.

Alternative: Since step is deterministic, strong normalization is equivalent to "there is no infinite reduction sequence" (well-foundedness of step). The standard proof of "Beta-validity": if t is a value abstraction in V and u ∈ R_T1 then app t u ∈ R_T2; and general R is closed under "expansion": if t --> t' and R t' then R t (because SN is closed under expansion given SN of reduct... need SN t: SN(t) from t --> t' and SN t').

This is getting complicated but manageable. Let me look for a simpler formulation for CBV SN which is known to work: the "Girard-Tait" reducibility for call-by-value can be defined as:

R_Bool(t) := SN t
R_{A->B}(t) := SN t /\ forall u, R_A(u) -> R_B(app t u)

This is the usual definition in CBV SN proofs (e.g., in "Semantics of Programming Languages" notes). The key lemmas:

1. SN is closed under reduction: SN t and t --> t' implies SN t'. (trivial by inversion)
2. SN is closed under expansion: SN t' and t --> t' implies SN t. (constructor).
3. All SN terms either are values or step? Not exactly — neutral terms like app of two values? In CBV app v1 v2 with v1 a value... if v1 is a value non-abstraction (true, false), then app true v doesn't step at all! It's stuck. And such terms are SN (no steps). Typed terms don't get stuck but untyped ones do. So the candidate definition "SN t" for base includes stuck terms. For arrow type, t could be a stuck term like `fvar x` or `app true...`; it won't be typable, but in the definition of R_arrow we include all SN terms closed under application with reducible arguments. Hmm, for fundamental theorem, terms of arrow type are either fvar x (with x's type arrow, then sigma x should be in R_arrow), or app, or abs. So the fvar case needs substitution env gives R. Then app case: t1 has type A->B, t2 type A; by induction R (subst t1) and R (subst t2); then R_arrow includes the application directly. Great.

The abs case needs: R_arrow (tm_abs A t) after substitution, i.e., SN (abs ...) and forall u in R_A, R_B (app (abs..) u).

Definitions:

```coq
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition subst1 x u t := subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else tm_fvar y) t.
```

Alternative: Since the language is deterministic CBV STLC with only Bool, maybe use the fact that strong normalization can be proven via "hereditarily normalizing" with a much simpler relation:

Define `R T t := SN t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> R T t')`? That's just SN plus closure... circular.

Another classic approach: interpret types as sets of strongly normalizing terms closed under expansion, with arrow defined via application. We can define the logical relation *inductively* or via "candidates" with an explicit "expand" closure:
- CR_Bool := { t | SN t }.
- CR_Arrow := { t | SN t /\ forall u ∈ CR_A, app t u ∈ CR_B }.
Then prove:
(i) CR closed under step (easy for base; arrow by induction on the type using ST_App1 with lc u... need lc).

Hmm. Let's revisit the definition. Perhaps a better logical relation for CBV uses the *value relation* as primitive and defines R via multi-step value reducts, which handles beta and applications more compositionally. Let me look at known approaches.

Known: For CBV STLC, one standard logical relation is:
- `V_Bool = {true, false}`.
- `V_{A→B} = {λx.t | for all v ∈ V_A, [v/x]t ∈ R_B}`.
- `R_A = {t | t is SN and every value v such that t ⇓ v satisfies V_A}` where ⇓ is the big-step evaluation relation (or multi-step to value).
Then the Fundamental Theorem is proven by induction on typing, with a substitution lemma for R.

This is quite clean because:
- T_App: t1 ∈ R_{A→B}, t2 ∈ R_A. Need app t1 t2 ∈ R_B. Show by induction on the evaluation of... Hmm, using big-step values: if app t1 t2 ⇓ v then there exist v1, v2 with t1 ⇓ v1, t2 ⇓ v2, app v1 v2 ⇓ v and v1 ∈ V_{A→B}. Then v1 = λx.b and [v2/x]b ∈ R_B from V_arrow; and app (λx.b) v2 ⇓ [v2/x]b ⇓ v. Need all value reducts... This uses the evaluation relation and a determinism/SN argument.
- SN of app t1 t2: if t1, t2 SN and t1 ∈ R... hmm, need similar analysis but with small steps.

Maybe a hybrid: Since we're proving strong normalization (SN), we can use the fact that a term is SN iff all its reducts are SN; and use the value-reduct characterization.

Take the definition:
```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop
with V : ty -> tm -> Prop := ...
```
Actually maybe define R as the *least* set closed under certain rules, or define R via "all reducts are in V". Let's define:

```
Definition vred (t v : tm) := multi step t v /\ value v.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  lc t /\ SN t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v
with V (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists t', t = tm_abs A t' /\ lc t /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open t' u)
  end.
```
Hmm, V_Bool = {true,false} is restrictive: R_Bool t requires all value reducts to be true or false. Ill-typed but SN terms like `abs` reduce to themselves (value) which is not true/false, so R_Bool(abs) fails. Then the "stuck terms are in all R" lemma fails for values. But do we need it? In the beta lemma, app (abs) u with u stuck: u is either a value or not. If u is genuinely stuck non-value, app(abs)u is stuck non-value: R T2 requires SN ✓, lc ✓, and all value reducts — there are none (no steps at all), so vacuously true! Great. If u is a value but "wrong" value? u ∈ R T1; if T1 = Bool, V_Bool u from R_Bool u and u value implies u ∈ {true,false}. If T1 = Arrow, V_arrow u means u = abs A t' with semantic body. So reducible values are canonical! That's the point of the V-based definition: R_Bool doesn't include non-canonical values like abs, but we may not need that. Let's check whether the definition is well-founded and the lemmas go through.

Key properties:
1. R implies lc, SN, and V for value reducts.
2. R closed under step / multi-step: if R T t and t -->* t', then R T t'. lc: step preserves lc. SN: closed. Value reducts of t' are value reducts of t. ✓ Very easy!
3. V implies value and lc. V_Bool: true/false are values and lc. V_Arrow: abs is a value if lc (lc of abs = lc_at 1 body). ✓
4. R of a canonical value? If value v and V T v then R T v? R T v needs: lc ✓ (from V), SN v (value and lc => SN? A value has no steps? tm_abs is a value but might it step? Step rules only conclude app steps; a bare abs doesn't step. true/false don't. So values are SN by SN_intro with no reducts — need lemma `value_SN`). And value reducts of v: v -->* w with value w. Since v is a value, does v -->* w imply w = v? multi_refl gives v; multi_step requires a step from v, impossible. So w = v, and V T v given. Thus R T v. ✓ Nice, no need for type-directed values being canonical.

5. "Stuck non-value SN terms are in R T": if t is not a value and has no steps (or more generally SN and all reducts...?). For the beta lemma, when u is stuck non-value, app (abs) u has no steps (abs is a value; u not value and no step), so it's SN by SN_intro with no reducts, lc, and no value reducts (since no steps at all, multi step only refl, giving app itself, which is not a value). So R T2 (app abs u) ✓ directly, no type induction needed! Wait: value reducts: multi step (app abs u) v with value v. Since no steps from app abs u, v = app abs u, which is an app, not a value. Contradiction. So vacuously true. 

6. Beta with u value: app (abs) u --> open t u. We need R T2 (app abs u): lc ✓; SN: open t u is R T2 hence SN; app's reducts: only open t u (since u value, abs value → ST_AppAbs; ST_App2 needs u --> u' impossible as u value? Wait values can't step ✓). So SN via SN_intro with the one reduct. Value reducts of app abs u: multi step to a value v. Since the only step is to open t u, multi is either refl or open t u -->* v. Refl gives app abs u not a value. So v is a value reduct of open t u, hence V T2 v by R T2(open t u). ✓ This works cleanly!

7. Beta with u non-value but stepping (SN): app abs u --> app abs u' for u --> u'. By induction on SN u, R T2 (app abs u'). Then need R T2 (app abs u) from the step app abs u --> app abs u'... We can't just use closure under step (that's forward). We need "R closed under expansion" or direct: lc ✓; SN: reducts of app abs u are: app abs u' (via ST_App2, for any u --> u') and open t u (via ST_AppAbs, only if u is a value — u is non-value in this case so no). So SN needs SN(app abs u') for all u' — by IH ✓. Value reducts: multi step (app abs u) v value. Path either refl (not value) or first step to app abs u' then value reduct of that, which is in V T2 by R T2(app abs u') from IH. ✓ So R T2(app abs u) by IH on SN u, without needing expansion lemma! Because we analyze the specific reducts.

But wait, the IH: for u' with u --> u', we get R T1 u' (from R_step) and then R T2 (app abs u'). Yes! And in case u is a value, no IH needed; in case u is non-value, every step of app abs u is ST_App2 with u --> u'. Note: to know that ST_AppAbs cannot occur, we need u not a value. So we DO need to case on whether u is a value (decidable). Hmm, is value decidable? For this syntax yes: value t is either abs T t with lc (decidable?) — lc_at is decidable too. But proving decidability in Coq requires some work. Alternatively use classical `value u \/ ~ value u` via `classical`. Is using classical logic acceptable? The task forbids added axioms. `Classical_Prop.classical` is an axiom in the standard library (classical axiom). Many benchmark solutions use `Classical` freely. The instruction says "must not use Admitted, added axioms, unsafe flags, or changes to the supplied language definitions and theorem statements." Using Coq's standard library classical axiom is an "axiom" in some sense, but it's part of the standard library, not "added". Hmm, risky. Let's see if we can avoid it.

Alternative: prove decidability of value and step by structural recursion. Value: 
```
Definition value_dec : forall t, {value t} + {~ value t}.
```
value t cases: abs T t requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t) which is decidable by induction on t; etc. Doable. But maybe we can avoid case on value entirely by a different reduction analysis: For app (abs T1 t) u, the possible steps are ST_App2 (u --> u') and ST_AppAbs (value u). We want to show R T2 (app abs u) assuming IH for all u' with step u u'. We need SN: each reduct app abs u' is SN by IH; reduct open t u (if value u) — do we have SN(open t u)? Hbody gives R T2 (open t u) only when R T1 u; we have that. So even without knowing whether u is a value, whenever a reduct of app abs u is open t u (which only happens if value u), we can use Hbody u Hu to get R T2(open t u) and hence SN. So SN can be proven without casing on value! Similarly value reducts: any value reduct of app abs u is either (a) refl — impossible as app isn't a value, or (b) obtained via a first step:
   - ST_AppAbs: reduct open t u, value reducts of the original are value reducts of open t u (multi transitively) → Hbody gives V.
   - ST_App2 with u --> u': value reducts are value reducts of app abs u' → IH.
So the inversion on the step relation gives us the case distinctions without needing decidability! Because in Coq, when we invert `step (app (abs T1 t) u) s'`, the cases are exactly ST_AppAbs, ST_App1, ST_App2, and ST_App1 requires step (abs T1 t) s1 which is impossible (values don't step); ST_AppAbs's arguments are constrained: the function part is abs T1 t, so T'=T1, t'=t, v=u; and gives value u.
For multi-step value reducts: invert the multi derivation. If multi_step (app abs u) s' v with step to s' and multi s' v. Then invert the first step into cases, and in each case use IH or Hbody. Since multi is an inductive, we can invert the first step by induction on the multi derivation: multi t v cases: refl (t=v, but t=app not value) or step t s' with multi s' v. So yes, no decidability needed.

Great, this V-based definition seems promising and avoids classical logic.

Now the main challenge shifts to the fundamental theorem with substitution. Let's detail.

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

Definition R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v)
```
with V defined mutually. In Coq, we can define by mutual Fixpoint:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v)
with V (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists t', t = tm_abs A t' /\ locally_closed t /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open t' u)
  end.
```
Wait, for V_Arrow the "lt" body property: should it be forall u, R A u -> R B (open t' u)? And lc t' means lc_at 1 t'. Note `locally_closed (tm_abs A t')` is equivalent.

But wait, R T for T=Bool: t = true or false would be too strong for R_Bool? No, R_Bool t := lc t /\ SN t /\ forall value reducts v, V_Bool v = (v=true \/ v=false). So R_Bool is essentially "SN and every value it reaches is true or false". Hmm, is `fvar x` in R_Bool? lc yes, SN yes, value reducts: multi (fvar x) v — fvar doesn't step so v = fvar x, not a value. ✓. Is `true` in R_Bool? value reducts: v=true ✓. Is `app (abs Bool true) true` in R_Bool? It steps to true, V ✓; SN ✓; lc ✓. Fine. What about a stuck non-value like `app true true`? lc ✓, SN ✓, value reducts: none (no steps, not a value) ✓. So R_Bool holds. What about `abs Bool true` at Bool type? value reducts: itself (a value) but V_Bool fails since abs ≠ true/false. So not in R_Bool. Is that a problem for fundamental theorem? A typable term of Bool type never is an abs (ill-typed), so no. But the "stuck lemma" we might want for non-values only, which holds vacuously. In beta lemma with u stuck non-value, app abs u is a non-value (app), so its only value reduct would be itself — not a value. ✓.

Wait, but there's a subtlety in the ST_AppAbs case of the beta SN proof: we get the reduct `open t u` with `value u`. We use Hbody u Hu : R T2 (open t u) — but Hbody assumes R T1 u, which we have. Good, R T2 gives SN.

Now the plan:

A. Basic lemmas about step and lc:
1. `lc_open_rec` / substitution of fvars preserves lc: if lc t and for all x, lc (sigma x) then lc (subst sigma t). More useful: subst_preserves_lc.
2. step preserves lc: step t t' -> lc t -> lc t'.
   Actually step might require lc hypotheses; let's prove by induction on step.
   - ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T t0) v; lc of app requires lc (abs T t0) = lc_at 1 t0, and lc v. Result open t0 v: need lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v t0) from lc_at 1 t0 and lc v. Standard lemma.
   - ST_App1: lc (app t1 t2) gives lc t1, lc t2; IH gives lc t1'; result lc (app t1' t2).
   - ST_App2: similar.
3. value doesn't step: value t -> ~ (t --> t'). By inversion on value and step; step conclusion always an app, but value true/false/abs are not apps. Actually inversion on step with t a value: step's conclusion is an app in all three rules, contradiction with t = abs/true/false. Simple.
4. values are SN: `value_SN`.
5. SN closed under step: SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'. By inversion on SN t (SN_intro) and applying the hypothesis.
6. SN preserved/closed under substitution? Maybe needed later.
7. multi_step transitivity and inversion.

B. Logical relation lemmas:
1. `R_lc`, `R_SN`, `R_V` (projections).
2. `V_value`, `V_lc`.
3. `R_step : R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'` by induction on T.
   - Bool: lc step; SN step; value reducts of t' are value reducts of t (multi trans). 
     To prove V_Bool for value reducts of t': if t' -->* v then t -->* v by multi_step on given step. ✓
   - Arrow: lc, SN via IH? Actually lc and SN are independent of T (R includes them at every type). For the forall part: forall v, multi t' v -> value v -> V_arrow v = exists body... We get from R_arrow t the same for t (multi from t). ✓ Wait, R_arrow t's third component is (forall v, multi t v -> value v -> V T v) which is the same V at type Arrow, and multi t v follows from step t t' and multi t' v. So R_step is trivial! Because the third component is uniform: value reducts of t' ⊆ value reducts of t. Indeed R T t = lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, multi t v -> value v -> V T v). So R_step follows directly for every T without induction! Great, since V is the same. So `R_step` is easy.
   Wait, but SN t' from SN t and step: yes.
   And R_multi similarly.
4. `V_SN`: V T v -> SN v. For Bool: true/false values → SN. Arrow: abs with lc → value → SN. Need lc v: V_arrow includes lc. Hmm, V_Bool v = v=true\/v=false gives value. ✓.
5. `V_R`? If V T v then R T v (for values with lc). Needed? Probably for Hbody in the abs case: we need R T2 (open t u) for all u ∈ R T1. That comes from the IH via substitution, not V_R. But maybe in beta: value u with R T1 u; Hbody gives R T2 (open t u). Fine.
6. The beta lemma `R_app_abs`: as designed, by induction on SN u. We'll need lc_at 1 t to know value (abs T t) for ST_App2's `value v1` premise. Also for ST_AppAbs premise locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t) — same.
   
   Statement:
   ```
   Lemma R_app_abs : forall u T1 T2 t,
     lc_at 1 t ->
     (forall w, R T1 w -> R T2 (open t w)) ->
     R T1 u ->
     R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u).
   ```
   Proof by induction on SN u. But we need to generalize over T1 T2 t before induction. We can get SN u from R T1 u.
   
   ```
   intros u T1 T2 t Hlc Hbody Hu.
   remember (SN u) ... 
   ```
   Better:
   ```
   assert (Hsn : strongly_normalizing u) by apply (R_sn _ _ Hu).
   revert T1 T2 t Hlc Hbody Hu.
   induction Hsn as [u IH].
   intros T1 T2 t Hlc Hbody Hu.
   ```
   Then goal `R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u)`.
   Since R T2 is a conjunction, split:
   - lc: lc_app, lc_abs (from Hlc: lc_at 1 t = locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t)), and R_lc Hu.
   - SN: prove `SN (app abs u)`:
     apply SN_intro; intros s' Hstep; invert Hstep.
       * ST_AppAbs: s' = open t u; apply R_sn with T2: apply Hbody; exact Hu. (Need the step's T and t parameters: ST_AppAbs T t v with lc abs, value v; conclusion `tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open t v`. Our app is `tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u`, so inversion should give T = T1, t = t, v = u.)
       * ST_App1: premise `tm_abs T1 t --> t1'`; contradiction via value_no_step (value abs holds by Hlc).
       * ST_App2: premise `value (tm_abs T1 t)` (need Hlc) and `u --> t2'`; conclusion s' = tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) t2'. Apply IH with: Hbody (same), R T1 t2' (from R_step Hu Hstep), Hlc.
     Hmm, the induction IH will be: `forall u', step u u' -> forall T1 T2 t, lc_at 1 t -> (forall w, R T1 w -> R T2 (open t w)) -> R T1 u' -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u')`. Good.
   - value reducts: forall v, multi step (app abs u) v -> value v -> V T2 v.
     Invert the multi derivation: 
       * multi_refl: v = app abs u, and value (app ...) — contradiction (no value constructor for app). Need lemma `value_app_absurd` or just inversion on value.
       * multi_step: step (app abs u) s' and multi s' v. Invert the step:
         - ST_AppAbs: s' = open t u. Then multi (open t u) v. From Hbody u Hu : R T2 (open t u), get its value-reduct component: forall v, multi (open t u) v -> value v -> V T2 v. Apply. ✓
         - ST_App1: absurd (abs doesn't step).
         - ST_App2 with u --> u': s' = app abs u'. Then use IH: R T1 u' from R_step Hu; R T2 (app abs u') from IH; then its value-reduct component applied to multi s' v. ✓
     
     Note: to use the third component of R T2 (app abs u') we need `R T2 (app abs u')`, which the IH provides. But careful: IH is exactly the full goal R T2 (app abs u'), so yes.

   This works! But wait, in the value-reducts branch, the inversion of `multi step (app abs u) v` gives the first step; for multi_step the premises are `step (app abs u) s'` and `multi step s' v`. Inverting step gives the cases. In ST_AppAbs case, s' = open t u and we have multi (open t u) v. Good.

   But hold on: induction on Hsn : strongly_normalizing u — the motive must be set so that IH is as desired. Since strongly_normalizing is inductive, `induction Hsn as [u IH]` where the goal after reverting is `forall T1 T2 t, ...`. The motive will be `fun u => forall T1 T2 t, lc_at 1 t -> ... -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u)`. Then IH : forall u', step u u' -> forall T1 T2 t, lc_at 1 t -> ... -> R T1 u' -> R T2 (app abs u'). Good. Note in the constructor, H : forall u', step u u' -> SN u', and the induction principle gives IH in the motive form. Yes.

   Need to double check `R T1 u'`: R_step Hu Hstep.

7. Now the fundamental theorem with substitution.

Define `subst` on fvars. Let me define:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...
Notation "[ sigma ] t" maybe.
```
Lemmas:
- subst preserves lc: if lc t and all sigma x lc, then lc (subst sigma t).
  Actually in the fundamental theorem, sigma maps each x to R (Gamma x) (sigma x) which gives lc. So we need forall x, lc (sigma x) — we have it pointwise wherever Gamma x is Some; for other x, sigma arbitrary, but t's fvars x satisfy Gamma x = Some T by typing... but subst applied to body t may contain fvars not in Gamma? For well-typed terms, all free fvars are in Gamma. But in the fundamental theorem we might not directly prove "fvars of t are in dom Gamma". Let's instead require globally forall x, lc (sigma x). Since sigma is arbitrary outside, we can choose it to map everything to lc terms. In the T_Abs case we extend sigma with u which is lc. So maintain invariant: forall x, lc (sigma x)? But the environment condition only constrains dom Gamma. We can simply add `forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)` as part of the sigma goodness (or just require always). For x not in gamma, no relation needed but lc is easy to maintain if the initial sigma maps everything to some lc term (e.g., tm_true). Hmm, but in the theorem application, we need an initial sigma for empty context; choose sigma := fun _ => tm_true, lc ✓.

  But when we extend sigma' = x |-> u; sigma (i.e., sigma' y = if y=x then u else sigma y), lc holds if u lc and sigma all lc. ✓.

  However, for T_Var case with Gamma x = Some T: we get R T (sigma x) from the env condition. But we need R T (subst sigma (fvar x)) = R T (sigma x). ✓.

  For T_Abs: the IH is for the opened body open t1 (fvar x) under context x|->T1;Gamma. The env condition for sigma' requires: for y with (x|->T1;Gamma) y = Some U, R U (sigma' y). We choose sigma' = fun y => if y =? x then u else sigma y. Given sigma good for Gamma and u ∈ R T1, sigma' good for x|->T1;Gamma ✓ (need eqb facts: update m x T y = if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else m y; note orientation: `update Gamma x T := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y`. And sigma' y = if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y. Same orientation, good.)
  
  Then IH gives R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
  We need this to equal R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u) where u is sigma' x. Let's prove a general lemma:
  `subst_open : forall sigma t u x, ~ In x (fv_tm t) -> subst (fun y => if eqb x y then u else sigma y) (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t)`? Hmm, but opening at what level? In T_Abs, `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` = open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1. The substitution lemma should be: subst sigma' (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k (sigma' x) (subst sigma t) under the condition that x is not free in t AND sigma' agrees with sigma on free vars of t (i.e., for y ≠ x, sigma' y = sigma y). Standard.
  
  But careful: `subst` goes under binders without shifting (since bvars are de Bruijn indices and substitution of fvars doesn't touch bvars). open_rec replaces bvar k with a term; subst sigma' commutes with open_rec if the inserted term's fvars are handled: subst sigma' (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k (sigma' x) (subst sigma t) requires? Let's check by induction on t:
   - bvar i: both sides: if i=k then sigma' x vs sigma' x ✓; else bvar i ✓.
   - fvar y: LHS = sigma' y; RHS = open_rec k (sigma' x) (sigma y). If y ≠ x and sigma' y = sigma y, RHS = open_rec k (sigma' x) (sigma y); this equals sigma y only if sigma y contains no bvar k... Hmm! open_rec on the substituted term could replace bvars inside sigma y. That's a problem: the RHS is open_rec k (sigma' x) (subst sigma t) where subst sigma t may contain bvar k inside substituted terms. For the equality to hold we'd need sigma y to have no bvar k. But in our use, k=0 and sigma y are arbitrary terms (possibly containing bvar 0? They're closed terms in practice—locally closed, so no bvars at all). Since all sigma y are locally closed, they contain no bvars, so open_rec on them is identity. Actually open_rec k u s where s locally closed (no bvars) = s (for any k, u). Good.
   
   So with `forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)` and x not free in t, we get the lemma:
   `subst_open_fvar : forall sigma t x u k,
       (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
       ~ In x (fvs t) ->
       subst (fun y => if eqb x y then u else sigma y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
       = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).`
   Hmm, wait do we even need x not free in t? If x occurs free in t, then LHS substitutes those occurrences with u, while RHS: subst sigma t replaces fvar x with sigma x (not u). So yes, need x not free in t. In the T_Abs case, choose x fresh from L and fvs(t1). But the typing premise is available for any x ∉ L. However, the IH is applied for that particular x. We need to instantiate the cofinite premise with an x that's fresh from L ∪ fvs(t1). Since fvs(t1) is finite decidable, we can pick such x. How to pick in Coq constructively? We can use `atom` = nat and construct a fresh nat: e.g., take max of L and the list of fvs of t1 plus 1. Need `fvs : tm -> list atom` and lemma `~ In x (fvs t)` for x = max+1. Manageable.

   Alternatively, avoid freshness issue by defining substitution semantics directly on `open t (fvar x)`: In the T_Abs case, the IH gives R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))) for a fresh x. If we instead set sigma' x = fvar x (i.e., environment maps x to itself) and sigma' agrees with sigma elsewhere, then subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = ? LHS = subst (fun y => if x=y then fvar x else sigma y) (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1). If x ∉ fvs(t1), this equals open_rec 0 (fvar x) (subst sigma t1) = open (subst sigma t1) (fvar x). Hmm, so we get R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) (fvar x)). That's reducibility of the body opened with the fvar x, not with arbitrary u. We still need to transfer from fvar x to arbitrary u, which is the same freshness/substitution issue. So no gain.

   Standard approach (SF): prove that the logical relation is closed under substituting fvars: if R T (open t (fvar x)) for all fresh x, then for all u, R T (open t u)? Or a lemma: `R_subst_fvar : R T t -> R T (subst1 x u t)` for u ∈ R (type of x)? We don't have types for arbitrary fvars though. Alternative: in the Abs case, we need forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u). Given for a *fresh* x, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) (fvar x)), can we derive for all u? There's a general "fvar-to-term" substitution lemma for R: if for fresh x, R T (open body (fvar x)), then for u ∈ R T1, R T (open body u). This is essentially: R is closed under substituting a fresh fvar by a reducible term. We can prove this by induction on T (using the beta lemma for arrow types?) Hmm, that's the classic "rename/substitution property of the logical relation". Since R for arrow involves applications, it's not obvious.

Let's think about the standard method: In locally nameless with cofinite quantification, the fundamental theorem is usually stated with an environment `sigma` (substitution for fvars) and the typing rule T_Abs is handled by picking fresh x and using the substitution lemma for opening. The logical relation needs to be closed under substitution: if R T t and u is a term with R (Gamma x) u ... hmm.

Actually, let me reconsider: maybe define the fundamental theorem differently, using *semantic typing of open terms* defined via all closing substitutions. Specifically:

`sem_type Gamma t T := forall sigma, (forall x T', Gamma x = Some T' -> R T' (sigma x)) -> R T (subst sigma t)`.

Then T_Abs: To prove sem_type Gamma (abs T1 t1) (T1->T2):
Take sigma good for Gamma. Want R (T1->T2) (subst sigma (abs T1 t1)) = R_arrow (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
Need for all u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).
Apply R_app_abs with Hbody : forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u). We still need this Hbody, obtained from IH with an appropriate sigma'.

IH: sem_type (x|->T1; Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2 for a fresh x.
Instantiate with sigma' = x |-> u; sigma. Good: for y with (x|->T1;Gamma) y = Some U, R U (sigma' y):
 - y = x: U = T1, sigma' x = u ∈ R T1 ✓.
 - y ≠ x: Gamma y = Some U, sigma' y = sigma y, sigma good ✓.
Get R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
Need R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u) = R T2 (open_rec 0 u (subst sigma t1)).
The substitution lemma gives subst sigma' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 u (subst sigma t1), provided x ∉ fvs(t1) and sigma locally closed and sigma' agrees with sigma off x. Wait, but there's a catch: in subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)), when we open t1 with fvar x, the fvar x is inserted at bvar 0 positions. But those positions are under binders; the substitution lemma needs the invariant that fvars substituted by sigma' don't contain bvars... Let me re-derive: define `subst sigma t` recursing under abs without shifting, replacing fvar y with sigma y. `open_rec k u t` replaces bvar k with u (no shifting). Since subst doesn't touch bvars and open doesn't touch fvars except inserting u, they commute if substitutions are bvar-free (locally_closed). Good.

So the required freshness: x ∉ fvs(t1). But the typing premise gives the judgment for any x ∉ L; we choose x = fresh(L ++ fvs(t1)). But careful: the premise of T_Abs in has_type is `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. We apply it to our chosen x. Then we get the IH for that x. But is the IH applicable? The fundamental theorem induction is over the typing derivation `has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`, which is a subderivation instantiated at x. In the proof by induction on has_type, the T_Abs case gives us the premise as `forall x, ~In x L -> has_type ...`, so for the IH we can use any x. Actually simpler: use `Nat.max` fold. Or find an atom not in a finite list via induction:
```
Lemma fresh_exists : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof. induction l as [|a l [x Hx]].
 - exists 0; simpl; auto.
 - destruct (Nat.eq_dec a x) as [->|Hne].
   + exists (S x); intros [H|H]; [lia | apply Hx; exact H].
   + exists x; intros [H|H]; [apply Hne; symmetry; exact H | apply Hx; exact H].
Qed.
```
Then use `destruct (fresh_exists (L ++ fvs t1)) as [x Hx]`, and then `~ In x L` and `~ In x (fvs t1)` from Hx. This avoids defining a fresh function. Constructive and simple.

But there's another subtlety: we need fvs of t1 and `~ In x (fvs t1)` to mean x doesn't occur free in t1. Define:
```
Fixpoint fvs (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvs t1 ++ fvs t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvs t1
  | tm_true => nil | tm_false => nil
  end.
Lemma fvs_correct : forall t x, In x (fvs t) <-> occurs_free x t? 
```
We can avoid a separate occurs predicate: just prove that if ~ In x (fvs t) then subst behaves. Define the substitution-open commutation lemma using the condition `~ In x (fvs t)` and prove by induction on t. Good.

So target proof:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t ∈ T -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (fundamental Gamma:=empty t:=t T:=T Hty idenv) as H.
  unfold env_good in H. 
  assert (Hg : env_good empty idenv). { split; [intro; apply lc_fvar | intros x U Hx; inversion Hx]. }
  specialize (H Hg).
  rewrite subst_idenv in H.
  apply R_sn in H. exact H.
Qed.
```

Let me also double check the T_Abs proof in detail with the environment extension and freshness:

Goal: R (T1 -> T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)) = R_arrow (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).

Unfold R at the goal (it's a Fixpoint; `simpl` should reduce since T1->T2 is a constructor). Goal becomes conjunction:
```
locally_closed (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) /\

Hmm, R's third component is `forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v`? Wait, in my earlier definition I wrote `forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v`, but I didn't include an intermediate value-reduct requirement correctly? Let me re-check: R T t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v). For arrow type T = A->B, V (A->B) v := exists t', v = tm_abs A t' /\ lc /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open t' u). Note that this third component of R does NOT directly give "forall u R A u -> R B (app t u)"! Because t might not be a value. Hmm! In the earlier plan, T_App needed R_arrow t to give R T2 (app t u) directly. But with the V-based definition, R_arrow t only gives info about value reducts of t. So T_App needs a lemma:

`R_app : forall A B t u, R (A->B) t -> R A u -> R B (tm_app t u)`.

Hmm right, this is a crucial lemma and it's nontrivial. Let's think: t ∈ R_arrow means lc, SN, and every value reduct of t is in V_arrow. u ∈ R_A means lc, SN, and every value reduct of u in V_A. We want R_B (app t u).

Intuitively: since t is SN, its reduction either ends in a value or gets stuck; value reducts v are V_arrow (canonical abstractions with semantic bodies). Then app t u reduces: while t reduces, app reduces; when t reaches value v = abs, then u reduces (via ST_App2 since v value) to a value w ∈ V_A, then beta to open body w ∈ R_B. So R_B (app t u) should hold. Proving this in Coq: by induction on SN t and SN u, analyzing multi/step reductions.

This is essentially the same complexity as the beta lemma but for arbitrary t ∈ R_arrow. Can we structure the definition to make T_App easier? Alternative definition of R for arrow that *includes* the application property directly:

R (A->B) t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, multi t v -> value v -> V (A->B) v) /\ (forall u, R A u -> R B (app t u)).

Then T_App is direct. But then V_arrow and R_arrow overlap; and proving abs ∈ R_arrow needs both the V part and the forall-app part (beta lemma), which is fine. And R_step closure still easy? If R_arrow t and t --> t', then:
- lc, SN ✓
- value reducts of t' ⊆ value reducts of t ✓
- forall u, R A u -> R B (app t' u): We have R B (app t u) and step (app t u) (app t' u); R closed under step (R_step at type B) would give R B (app t' u). But R_step is what we're proving! **Strategy: SN as well-founded induction with a "strongly normalizing application" lemma.**

I think the V-based (Option 2) approach is actually quite clean if we prove the key "R_app" lemma carefully. Let's try to formalize it.

Define:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  lc t /\ SN t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v
with V (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists t', t = tm_abs A t' /\ lc_at 1 t' /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open t' u)
  end.
```

Key lemmas:
(L1) R implies lc, SN; V implies value? For Bool, true/false are values. For Arrow, abs with lc is a value. Also V implies lc.
(L2) R closed under step and multi: easy as noted (value reducts monotone, lc step, SN step).
(L3) V implies R (for the value itself): important for T_App? Maybe.
(L4) R_app: R (A->B) t -> R A u -> R B (app t u).
(L5) beta lemma: lc abs + Hbody -> R B (app abs u).

Actually, (L4) can be derived from (L5)-(L3)-ish: t ∈ R_arrow means all value reducts of t are V_arrow (canonical abs with semantic body). If t has no value reduct (stuck while non-value), app t u is stuck? Hmm, t might step forever? No, t SN. If t is stuck non-value, app t u is stuck: R B by vacuity (lc, SN, no value reducts). If t reduces to a value v ∈ V_arrow, then app t u -->* app v u... but "multi app t u (app v u)" and then u reduces to value w, then beta. So R_B(app t u) can be shown by: lc ✓; SN(app t u) (needs analysis); value reducts of app t u are value reducts of app v w for value reducts v of t and w of u? Then R B (app v w) via beta lemma and V_arrow.

Then lc_open_inv (k=0) with u=fvar x (lc) gives lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). ✓. So instantiate the generic Hbody proof with u = tm_fvar x and prove R T1 (tm_fvar x) via `fvar_R`.
   - Then R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)):
     split lc: lc_abs with lc_at 1 ✓.
     split SN: value_SN (value_abs lc_abs) — value_abs requires locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body) which is lc_at 1 body ✓. So need lc.
     third: forall v, multi (abs) v -> value v -> V_arrow v. First show v = abs via `value_multi_self` (since abs is a value and multi from a value is refl): v = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1). Then V_arrow with witness (subst sigma t1): equality, lc_at 1, and forall u Hu, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u) = Hbody ✓.

6. Fundamental theorem induction. Note in T_App case: subst sigma (app t1 t2) = app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2). Apply R_app with IH1: R (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1) and IH2: R T1 (subst sigma t2). ✓.

7. Target: fundamental with identity env; subst id t = t; R_sn.

Potential issue: `subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)` — subst recurses under the binder without shifting and replaces fvars: result tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1). ✓ by definition.

Now let's also verify the fundamental theorem's T_Abs handling of IH. The IH from the induction principle:
`IH : forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, env_good (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)))`
Wait, actually the motive P Gamma t T := forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t). Hmm, but subtlety: `R T (subst sigma t)` where t is the term in the judgment. P is a predicate over (Gamma, t, T). Coq's induction will generate the motive with sigma universally quantified if we do `induction Hty` after intros? Let's be careful. We can also formulate the fundamental theorem in "sem_type" style and use a custom induction principle to be safe:

Define `sem_type Gamma t T := forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)` and prove:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> sem_type Gamma t T.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H as [...]; intros sigma Hg; ...
```
Here after `intros ... H`, goal is `sem_type Gamma t T`, which unfolds to a forall; `induction H` should generalize Gamma t T and the goal is sem_type..., so IH will be `sem_type ...` for the premises. In T_Abs case, the recursive premise is `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`, so the IH becomes `forall x, ~ In x L -> sem_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. Good.

But the third constructor's IH also includes the has_type? Coq's induction principle for has_type: the IH for a recursive argument of type `has_type ...` is the motive at those arguments, not including the has_type. So IH : forall x, ~In x L -> sem_type ... . Good.

Potential gotcha: The T_Abs constructor's premise is a function `forall x, ~In x L -> has_type ...`. The induction principle generated by Coq for such nested recursive occurrences: The recursive occurrence is under a binder (forall x) and an implication. Coq's induction principle (scheme) will produce IH : forall x, ~ In x L -> sem_type ... as long as the recursive occurrence is a direct argument after the forall? Actually, there's a known limitation: for inductive types with premises like `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`, Coq's automatically generated induction principle may not produce IHs under the binder correctly — it might require the entire premise as a hypothesis without IH, or it might produce `forall x, ~In x L -> sem_type...`. Let me recall SF's STLC_adt: they often define `has_type` with `(forall x, ~ In x L -> ...)` and prove lemmas by induction on has_type; Coq does generate the IH under the binder. I believe the scheme is like:
```
has_type_ind :
 forall P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop,
 (forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> P Gamma (tm_fvar x) T) ->
 (forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1,
    (forall x : atom, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) ->
    P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)) ->
 ...
```
Yes, the recursive occurrence under the forall gets an IH under the same forall. Good.

But the *motive* P is `fun Gamma t T => sem_type Gamma t T`, which requires the parameters to be generalizable. In `induction H`, Coq abstracts Gamma t T in the goal. Since the goal after `intros Gamma t T H` is `sem_type Gamma t T` (if we state the lemma with a Definition sem_type, the goal is `sem_type Gamma t T`; Coq should be able to abstract). Hmm, when sem_type is a Definition, `induction H` will unfold? It doesn't need to unfold; the motive is `fun Gamma t T => sem_type Gamma t T`. That's fine. But sometimes Coq fails to generalize because sem_type is not syntactically a variable? It should be fine: the goal is an application of sem_type to the variables.

To be safe, state the lemma with explicit forall sigma:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [...]; intros sigma Hg; ...
```
After intros Gamma t T H, goal is `forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`. `induction H` will abstract Gamma, t, T and introduce sigma in the motive? Coq's induction works on the goal, generalizing the conclusion over the variables; sigma is universally bound in the goal, so the motive is `fun Gamma t T => forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)` and IH will have `forall sigma`. I think Coq handles this: it reverts sigma? Actually Coq's `induction` does not automatically revert hypotheses that depend on the induction variables, but sigma doesn't depend on them; the goal `forall sigma, ...` is a product, and the motive can be that product type. The induction principle instantiates P with the product `fun Gamma t T => forall sigma, ...`? P must be `context -> tm -> ty -> Prop`, and the product is a Prop ✓. Coq constructs P from the goal by lambda-abstracting Gamma t T; the product over sigma remains inside. So yes IH: forall x, ~In x L -> (forall sigma, env_good (update...) sigma -> R T2 ...). Great.

Actually careful: in the T_Var case, the constructor argument Hx : Gamma x = Some T is not recursive. Fine.

Now the `env_good` in the T_Abs case: we need `env_good (update Gamma x T1) sigma'` where sigma' y = if eqb x y then u else sigma y. Let's define notation: update Gamma x T1 = fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T1 else Gamma y. And sigma' y = if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

env_good:
- lc part: forall y, locally_closed (sigma' y). Case on eqb x y: true → u lc (from Hu : R T1 u → R_lc); false → sigma lc (from Hg).
- R part: forall y U, update Gamma x T1 y = Some U -> R U (sigma' y).
  Let Hy : (if eqb x y then Some T1 else Gamma y) = Some U.
  Case eqb x y: true → Some T1 = Some U → U = T1; sigma' y = u; rewrite → R T1 u ✓.
  false → Gamma y = Some U; sigma' y = sigma y; apply Hg ✓.
  
  Need `Nat.eqb` reflection: `Nat.eqb_eq : forall n m, (n =? m) = true <-> n = m`, and `Nat.eqb_neq`. From stdlib Arith.PeanoNat, available as `Nat.eqb_eq`, `Nat.eqb_neq`. We imported Arith.PeanoNat. We can use `destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E; apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; ...` or use `Nat.eqb_eq` and `Nat.eqb_neq`.

Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

`SN_multi`: SN t -> multi t v -> SN v. By induction on multi.

Now `R_step`. Definition:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v)
with V (T : ty) (v : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists body,
      v = tm_abs A body /\
      lc_at 1 body /\
      (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.
```
Mutual Fixpoint on T. But `R`'s body mentions V at the same T, and V's body mentions R at subterms A and B. This is a valid mutual fixpoint with structural recursion on T? Coq requires the recursive calls to be on structurally smaller arguments; V T calls R A and R B where A, B are subterms of T ✓. R T calls V T — same argument, not smaller! In a mutual fixpoint, calling the other function on the *same* argument is allowed? The guard condition: for mutual fixpoints, each recursive call must decrease the principal argument, but calls to the other fixpoint on the same argument... I think Coq's guard checker requires each recursive call (including mutual) to be on a structurally smaller argument, EXCEPT it allows calls where the argument is the same if the function is the "other" one? Hmm, no. Consider:
```
Fixpoint even (n:nat) : bool := match n with 0 => true | S k => odd k end
with odd (n:nat) : bool := match n with 0 => false | S k => even k end.
```
Here even calls odd on k (smaller) ✓. If even called odd on the same n, that's not allowed.

Our R T calls V T on the same T. Is that allowed? In mutual recursion, the guard checker treats the block: a call from f to g must have a smaller argument? I believe the rule: for mutual fixpoints, recursive calls must be on a structural subterm of the argument of the *called* function; and there's a notion that the "principal argument" of the block is compared. A call to another function in the block on the same argument is not decreasing, so it would be rejected... Let me think of a counterexample: 
```
Fixpoint f (n:nat) : nat := match n with 0 => 0 | S k => g n end
with g (n:nat) : nat := match n with 0 => 0 | S k => f k end.
```
Is f (S k) => g (S k) accepted? I believe Coq rejects because the call to g with the same n isn't on a subterm. Hmm, but actually I recall that in mutual fixpoints, calls to another function can be on the same argument if that function's own principal argument decreases elsewhere? The guard checker uses a "subterm" relation per function, and cross-calls need the argument to be a subterm of the caller's argument. Same argument is not a proper subterm. So R calling V on same T likely rejected.

Alternative: define V independently of R? V_arrow mentions R A and R B. So the mutual dependency is inherent. We can instead define R using V, and V using R — maybe define them as a single Fixpoint returning a pair of Props? Like:
```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) : (tm -> Prop) * (tm -> Prop) :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => (fun t => lc t /\ SN t, fun v => v = true \/ v = false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      let (RA, VA) := RV A in
      let (RB, VB) := RV B in
      (fun t => lc t /\ SN t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (snd (RV T) v)?, ...)
```
But R at T needs V at T = snd (RV T), which is being defined — recursive call on T itself again. Hmm.

Alternative: avoid mutual recursion by defining R and V in a single Fixpoint where R T includes the V-condition inline via a helper that pattern matches on T (i.e., inline the definition of V into R, duplicating the arrow case). We can define a single Fixpoint `R` that at each T returns the conjunction and internally defines the "value condition" structurally. For the arrow case, R (A->B) needs:
- forall v, multi t v -> value v -> V_{A->B} v,
where V_{A->B} v = exists body, v = abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u).
This mentions R A and R B, which are recursive calls on smaller types ✓ (from within R at T = A->B). And the V-condition at type T itself: for base, V_Bool is just a formula; for arrow, the formula above. So we can inline V:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
          (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.
```
But this duplicates the definition of R for the arrow case: the third component at T=A->B is about value reducts, and R A and R B are recursive calls on subterms ✓. No self-call on T. 

Then define `V` as a separate Fixpoint (not mutually recursive) after R:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\ forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u)
  end.
```
And a lemma `R_V : forall T t, R T t -> forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v`. Since R's third component is defined inline, we prove this by cases on T; for arrow the inline formula matches V's definition. ✓.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=

R_step:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T. induction T as [| A IH_A B IH_B]; intros t t' HR Hst.
  - (* Bool *) destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]. split; [apply step_lc with t; auto | split].
    + apply SN_step with t; auto.
    + intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v). 
      * apply multi_step with t'; auto. (* multi t v from step t t' and multi t' v *) 
      * auto.
  - (* Arrow *) simpl in *. destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]. split...
    + lc.
    + SN_step.
    + intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v); [apply multi_step with t' | ]; auto.
Qed.
```
Wait, the value-reducts formula in R Arrow is the exists-formula; Hval : forall v, multi t v -> value v -> exists body, ...; we just need multi t v, which we get from step t t' + multi t' v. ✓. Also, for the arrow case, no need for IH_A/IH_B at all! R_step doesn't need structural induction since the value-reduct condition is just monotone under reduction and lc/SN are type-independent. Indeed we can prove R_step uniformly:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' HR Hst.
  destruct T as [| A B].
  - destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]; split; [apply step_lc with t; auto | split; [apply SN_step with t; auto |]].
    intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v); [|auto]. apply multi_step with t'; auto.
  - destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]; split; [...]. intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v); [|auto]. apply multi_step with t'; auto.
Qed.
```
Even better: we can prove a "uniform" statement using the fact that all R T share lc and SN and reduction-monotone value condition. But the destruct on T works.

Then R_multi similarly by induction on multi using R_step.

Now the R_app proof. Let me write it fully in a way that should work:

```
Lemma R_app : forall A B t u,
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) t -> R A u -> R B (tm_app t u).
Proof.
  intros A B t u HRt Hu.
  assert (Hsnt : strongly_normalizing t) by apply (R_sn _ _ HRt).
  revert A B u HRt Hu.
  induction Hsnt as [t IH].
  intros A B u HRt Hu.
  destruct HRt as [Hlct [Hsnt Hvalt]].
  split.
  - (* lc *) apply lc_app; [exact Hlct | apply (R_lc _ _ Hu)].
  - split.
    + (* SN *) apply SN_intro. intros s' Hst.
      inversion Hst as [T body v Hlcabs Hvval | t1 t1' t2 Hst1 Hlct2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv1 Hst2].
      * (* ST_AppAbs: t = abs T body, u = v *) subst.
        (* Hvalt : forall v, multi t v -> value v -> exists body, ... *)
        (* We want SN (open body u). Get V_arrow for t = abs T body from Hvalt with multi_refl and Habsval. *)
        assert (Habs : value (tm_abs T body)) by (apply v_abs; assumption). (* Hlcabs : locally_closed (tm_abs T body) *)
        destruct (Hvalt (tm_abs T body) (multi_refl _ _) Habs) as [body' [Heq [Hlcb Hbody]]].
        inversion Heq; subst. (* body' = body, T = A *)
        apply (R_sn _ _ (Hbody u Hu)).
      * (* ST_App1 *) 
        apply IH with (A:=A) (B:=B) (u:=u); [exact Hst1 | apply (R_step _ _ _ HRt Hst1) | exact Hu].
        (* Wait: IH t1' Hst1 A B u ...: IH : forall t', step t t' -> forall A B u, R (A->B) t' -> R A u -> R B (app t' u). *)
      * (* ST_App2: t = v1, step u t2', s' = app v1 t2' *)
        subst v1. (* t = v1? careful: v1 is a variable and premise value v1; term is tm_app v1 t2, unified with tm_app t u so v1 = t? Actually the step has source tm_app v1 t2 and our source is tm_app t u; inversion gives v1 = t? Hmm, inversion of step with our term: the third constructor matches with v1 = t, t2 = u, t2' = s'? Wait the conclusion is tm_app v1 t2 --> tm_app v1 t2'. Unifying tm_app v1 t2 with tm_app t u gives v1 = t, t2 = u; and s' = tm_app v1 t2' = tm_app t t2'.         (* We need R B (app t t2') from beta lemma. t is a value (Hv1), and R_arrow t. *)
        assert (Habs : value t) by assumption. (* Hv1 *)
        destruct (Hvalt t (multi_refl _ _) Habs) as [body [Heq [Hlcb Hbody]]].
        inversion Heq; subst. (* t = tm_abs A body *)
        apply (R_sn _ _ (R_app_abs A B body t2' Hlcb Hbody (R_step _ _ _ Hu Hst2))).
    + (* value reducts *)
      intros v Hm Hvval.
      inversion Hm as [x | x y z Hst Hm']; subst.
      * inversion Hvval. (* value (app ...) *)
      * inversion Hst as [T body w Hlcabs Hwval | t1 t1' t2 Hst1 Hlct2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv1 Hst2].

           apply (R_V B (Hbody u Hu) Hm' Hvval).
        -- (* ST_App1 *) 
           (* R B (app t1' u) from IH, then R_V *)
           apply (R_V B (IH _ Hst1 A B u (R_step _ _ _ HRt Hst1) Hu) Hm' Hvval).
        -- (* ST_App2 *)
           assert (Habs : value t) by assumption.
           destruct (Hvalt t (multi_refl _ _) Habs) as [body [Heq [Hlcb Hbody]]].
           inversion Heq; subst.
           apply (R_V B (R_app_abs A B body t2' Hlcb Hbody (R_step _ _ _ Hu Hst2)) Hm' Hvval).
Qed.
```

Wait, there's an issue in the ST_AppAbs case for R_app: we derived V_arrow for t using Hvalt with v := t, which requires `value t`. `R_V`: as discussed:
```
Lemma R_V : forall T t v, R T t -> multi step t v -> value v -> V T v.
Proof.

Qed.
```

Actually, maybe define R and V more conveniently. Let me think about how to define V so that `R_V` is definitional.

Alternative: define a single function `RV T` that returns a record/Prod of (R-predicate, V-predicate) but with R's value condition referencing the V at the same T? That self-reference is the issue.

Inline duplication approach:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
          (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.

Definition V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
        (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.
```
R_V: after destruct T, R T t reduces with simpl to lc /\ SN /\ (V-formula). But V T v also reduces to the same formula. Since both are syntactic matches over the same constructor, `simpl` should give syntactically equal propositions. So:
```
Lemma R_V : forall T t v, R T t -> multi step t v -> value v -> V T v.
Proof.
  intros T. destruct T as [| A B]; intros t v HR Hm Hv.
  - simpl in *. destruct HR as [_ [_ H]]. apply H; auto.
  - simpl in *. destruct HR as [_ [_ H]]. apply H; auto.
Qed.
```
`simpl in *` might unfold R and V. Since T is a constructor in each branch, yes.

Hmm, but simpl might also unfold `open`, `multi`, etc. unnecessarily. Should be OK.

Wait, there's another subtlety: in the definition of R's arrow case, `R A u` and `R B (open body u)` are recursive calls. Coq's guard checker: the recursive argument must be a subterm of T. Here T = Ty_Arrow A B, and A, B are subterms of the term `Ty_Arrow A B`? In Coq's guard condition, for a Fixpoint on argument T, recursive calls must have an argument that is a *subterm* of the original argument (in the sense of the inductive type's subterm relation). A and B are variables bound by the pattern match on T (when T = Ty_Arrow A B), so they are considered subterms of T. ✓. But they appear inside a `forall` and an `exists` — that's fine.

However, there's a catch: the match on T appears inside a conjunction and under a match; Coq's guard checker may have trouble if the recursive calls are under a match that is not directly on the recursive argument? Actually, R's body: `locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ match T with ... R A ... R B ... end`. The match is on T, the decreasing argument ✓. The recursive calls appear in the branches. This is standard. Should pass.

Now another consideration: R is a Fixpoint with T as the decreasing argument, but does Coq infer the decrease from the match? The match on T is inside a conjunction. The guard checker looks for recursive calls in the body; it needs to know they occur under a match on T. Since the match is on T syntactically, yes. But the outer structure `lc /\ SN /\ match...` — recursive calls are inside the third conjunct. The guard checker should handle this.

Hmm, but maybe Coq requires the match to be the "outermost" case analysis for the recursion? No, guard checking tracks the "rel" context: under a match on the recursive argument, branches can call recursively with subterms. Since the match is directly on T, fine.

So we need Hlcbody (lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1)) as discussed.

```
Lemma R_true : R Ty_Bool tm_true.
Proof.
  split; [apply lc_true | split; [apply value_SN, v_true |]].
  intros v Hm Hv. rewrite (value_multi_self _ _ Hm v_true). left; reflexivity.
Qed.

  - intros v Hm Hv. destruct T as [| A B]; simpl.

Lemma fresh_exists : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| a l [x Hx]].
  - exists 0. intros []. 
  - destruct (Nat.eq_dec a x) as [-> | Hne].
    + exists (S x). intros [H | H].
      * apply Nat.nlt_succ_diag_l? H : S x = x. lia.
      * apply Hx; exact H.
    + exists x. intros [H | H].
      * apply Hne. symmetry; exact H.
      * apply Hx; exact H.
Qed.
```

Define:
```
Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

- abs: f_equal.

Actually for app: `simpl` gives `tm_app (subst_tm idenv t1) (subst_tm idenv t2) = tm_app t1 t2`; `rewrite IHt1, IHt2; reflexivity`. Now, the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case needs the substitution lemma with sigma' = fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y. Let me define a helper:

Definition extend (x : atom) (u : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=

Substitution lemma:
```
Lemma subst_tm_open_fvar : forall t sigma x u k,
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  ~ In x (fvs t) ->
  subst_tm (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)

```
Wait, `extend x u sigma` unfolds to fun y => if eqb x y then u else sigma y. But there's a subtlety: in the fvar case y, LHS = extend x u sigma y = if eqb x y then u else sigma y. RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y). We need: if y = x, LHS = u but the freshness hypothesis excludes y = x (because y = x means x ∈ fvs (fvar x)). If y ≠ x, LHS = sigma y and RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y) = sigma y since sigma y locally closed. But wait, `open_rec k u (sigma y)`: since sigma y is locally closed (no bvars), open_rec does nothing for any k ✓. Good.

However, hold on: `open_rec k u (sigma y)` — our lc_open_rec_id lemma says lc_at n t -> n ≤ k -> open_rec k u t = t. With n=0, need 0 ≤ k ✓. Good.

In the abs case, the recursion needs the IH with k := S k, and the freshness hypothesis is preserved (fvs (tm_abs T t1) = fvs t1). ✓.

Now, one more consideration: the extend function and eqb orientation. update Gamma x T1 y = if Nat.eqb x y then Some T1 else Gamma y. extend x u sigma y = if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y. Good, same orientation.

Now let's consider the T_Abs proof once more in detail with the induction hypothesis.

Setup:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty as [Gamma x T Hx
                | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 IH
                | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2
                | Gamma
                | Gamma].
```
Hmm, the constructor argument names: T_Var : forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> has_type Gamma (tm_fvar x) T. T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2). T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, has_type Gamma t1 (T1->T2) -> has_type Gamma t2 T1 -> has_type Gamma (app t1 t2) T2.

```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.

  - apply lc_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; auto.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IH. lia.

  - apply lc_false.

Lemma lc_open_rec_id : forall t n k u, lc_at n t -> n <= k -> open_rec k u t = t.

lc_open_inv:
```
Lemma lc_open_inv : forall t k u, locally_closed u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
```
Wait, we need `lc_at 0 u` (locally_closed), and the monotonicity to lc_at k u. Let's state:
```
Lemma lc_open_inv : forall t k u, lc_at 0 u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k u Hu H; simpl in H.
  - (* bvar i *) destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst i. apply lc_bvar. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion H; subst. apply lc_bvar. lia. 
      (* H : lc_at k (tm_bvar i) gives i < k; need i < S k *)
  - apply lc_fvar.

Lemma step_lc : forall t t', step t t' -> locally_closed t -> locally_closed t'.

    apply lc_open_rec with (k := 0); assumption.
  - (* ST_App1 *)

    apply lc_app. apply IH. exact Hlc1. exact Hlc2'.
  - (* ST_App2 *)

Qed.
```

- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> step t2 t2' -> step (tm_app v1 t2) (tm_app v1 t2')

step_ind : forall P : tm -> tm -> Prop,

```coq
(** ** Auxiliary lemmas *)

Lemma lc_at_mono : forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros t k H. induction H; intros k' Hle.
  - apply lc_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; auto.
  - apply lc_abs. apply IH. lia.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.

Lemma lc_open_rec : forall t k u,
  lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Proof.
  induction t as [i | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | |];
    intros k u Ht Hu; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst i. exact Hu.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E.
      inversion Ht; subst. apply lc_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - inversion Ht; subst. apply lc_app; auto.
  - inversion Ht; subst. apply lc_abs.
    apply IH with (k := S k) (u := u).
    + assumption.
    + apply lc_at_mono with (k := k); auto. lia.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.
```
Hmm, in the app case, `inversion Ht; subst` gives lc_at (S k) t1 and lc_at (S k) t2. Then `apply lc_app; auto` uses IH1 and IH2. But `auto` might not use them with the right k,u. The goal after inversion: lc_at k (tm_app (open_rec k u t1) (open_rec k u t2)). `apply lc_app` gives two goals: lc_at k (open_rec k u t1) and lc_at k (open_rec k u t2). `apply IH1 with (k:=k) (u:=u); assumption`? Similarly for abs.

Actually, there's a subtlety with `inversion Ht; subst` in the abs case: Ht : lc_at (S k) (tm_abs T t1) gives lc_at (S (S k)) t1. `inversion Ht; subst` should produce it. lc_open_rec_id:
```coq
Lemma lc_open_rec_id : forall t n k u,
  lc_at n t -> n <= k -> open_rec k u t = t.
Proof.
  induction t as [i | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | |];
    intros n k u Ht Hnk; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst i.
      exfalso. inversion Ht; subst. lia.
    + reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IH1 with (n := n) (k := k) (u := u); inversion Ht; assumption.
    + apply IH2 with (n := n) (k := k) (u := u); inversion Ht; assumption.
  - f_equal.
    apply IH with (n := S n) (k := S k) (u := u).
    + inversion Ht; assumption.
    + lia.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Hmm, in the app case, `inversion Ht; assumption` — inversion Ht produces the sub-hypotheses and possibly substitutes; then assumption should find lc_at n t1. But `inversion Ht` may leave multiple subgoals or clear Ht. Let's be more careful:
```
  - inversion Ht as [k0 i Hi | k0 x0 | k0 a b Ha Hb | k0 T0 t0 Hbody | k0 | k0]; subst.
```
This pattern must match the constructors of lc_at: lc_bvar (2 args + proof), lc_fvar (2 args), lc_app (3 args + 2 proofs), lc_abs (3 args + 1 proof), lc_true (1), lc_false (1). For the app case, unifying lc_at n (tm_app t1 t2) with lc_app k0 a b Ha Hb gives k0 = n, a = t1, b = t2. So pattern `[k0 i Hi | k0 a | k0 a b Ha Hb | k0 T0 t0 Hbody | k0 | k0]`. Hmm, but in the app case, only the app constructor is possible, so inversion gives one goal; using the full pattern with 6 alternatives might fail if inversion only generates the possible case? `inversion Ht as [...]` supports patterns for each possible constructor; Coq will complain if the number of patterns doesn't match the number of goals generated. Inversion of lc_at n (tm_app t1 t2) generates exactly one goal (the app constructor), since the term's head is tm_app and only lc_app can conclude that. So we should write `inversion Ht as [k0 a b Ha Hb]; subst`? I'm not 100% sure inversion eliminates impossible constructors automatically and generates exactly one subgoal. Yes, inversion on an inductive whose conclusion's head constructor is determined will only keep the matching constructor(s), solving the rest by discrimination. So for tm_app, one case. For tm_abs, one case. For tm_bvar, one case. Etc.

But in the bvar case with `subst i := k`, the term is tm_bvar k and lc_at n (tm_bvar k) inverts to lc_bvar n k (k < n). Fine.

To avoid fragile inversion patterns, use the projection lemmas? But those are defined for lc_at at any k; lc_app_inv and lc_abs_inv can be used. Let's use them in lc_open_rec_id and lc_open_inv to simplify:
- app case: `destruct (lc_app_inv n t1 t2 Ht) as [H1 H2]`.
- abs case: `destruct (lc_abs_inv n T t1 Ht) as Hbody`? lc_abs_inv returns lc_at (S n) t1.
- bvar case: need inversion; there's no projection. We can prove a lemma `lc_bvar_inv : forall k i, lc_at k (tm_bvar i) -> i < k`, by inversion. Similarly `lc_fvar` doesn't need. Let's add `lc_bvar_inv`.

Similarly for lc_open_inv.

Lemma lc_open_rec : forall t k u,
  lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).

Now fvs:
```coq
Fixpoint fvs (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvs t1 ++ fvs t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvs t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.
```

Lemma fresh_exists : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.

  induction l as [| a l [x Hx]].

    + subst a. exists (S x). intros [H | H].
      * apply Nat.succ_neq in H?

      * apply Hx; exact H.

Now R and V definitions:
```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
          (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.

Definition V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      exists body, v = tm_abs A body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
        (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u))
  end.
```
Note: within R's definition, the recursive calls R A and R B are under the match on T. The Fixpoint's decreasing argument is T. Will Coq accept? `R A` where A is a variable bound in the pattern match on T = Ty_Arrow A B. The guard checker should recognize A as a subterm. Let's hope.

Hmm, one potential issue: In the arrow branch, the expression `match T with ... end` is inside `/\`; the recursive calls `R A` and `R B` appear inside a `forall` and `exists`. Guard condition should be fine.

Now the projection lemmas:
```coq

Qed.
```
Hmm, `simpl in H` with T a constructor: R (Ty_Bool) t should reduce. Then H : locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ ...; `apply H`? To prove locally_closed t from a conjunction, `apply H` is not valid; H is a conjunction. Use `exact (proj1 H)`. Similarly:
```coq
Lemma R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t.

```

```coq
Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof. intros T t H. destruct T; simpl in H; exact (proj1 (proj2 H)). Qed.
```
H : lc /\ SN /\ X; proj2 H : SN /\ X; proj1 (proj2 H) : SN ✓.

```coq
Lemma R_V : forall T t v, R T t -> multi step t v -> value v -> V T v.
Proof.
  intros T t v H Hm Hv. destruct T as [| A B]; simpl in *.
  - exact (proj2 (proj2 H) v Hm Hv).
  - exact (proj2 (proj2 H) v Hm Hv).
Qed.
```
After destruct T and simpl in *, H : lc t /\ SN t /\ X where X matches V T v's definition. In Bool case, V Bool v = (v = true \/ v = false). proj2(proj2 H) : forall v, multi... -> value v -> (v = true \/ v = false); apply to v Hm Hv ✓.
In Arrow case, V (Arrow A B) v = exists body, ...; proj2(proj2 H) : forall v, multi... -> value v -> exists body, ...; apply ✓.
But `simpl in *` may simplify H and the goal. The goal is V T v; after destruct T and simpl, it should reduce to the same formula. `exact (proj2 (proj2 H) v Hm Hv)` should typecheck if both sides are syntactically equal. Since V is a Definition with a match, `simpl` should unfold. Might need `unfold V; simpl`. Let's use `simpl in *; ...`. Hmm, `simpl in *` unfolds R in H; but does it unfold V in the goal? V (Ty_Bool) v should reduce by simpl. Yes.

Actually, there's a subtlety: `simpl` may not unfold `V` because V is a Definition (not Fixpoint) whose body is a match; simpl unfolds Definitions only if they're reducible? `simpl` does delta-reduction for transparent constants when it exposes a match/iota. V is a Definition, so simpl can unfold it. Should be fine. If not, use `unfold V; simpl`.

Lemma value_multi_self : forall t v, multi step t v -> value t -> v = t.
Proof.

Goal: value x -> z = x. intros Hv; exfalso; value_no_step x y Hv Hst ✓. Good.

```coq
Lemma fvar_no_step : forall x t', ~ step (tm_fvar x) t'.
Proof. intros x t' H. inversion H. Qed.

Lemma multi_fvar : forall x v, multi step (tm_fvar x) v -> v = tm_fvar x.
Proof.
  intros x v Hm. induction Hm as [z | x0 y z Hst Hm IH].
  - reflexivity.
  - exfalso. exact (fvar_no_step x y Hst).
Qed.
```
Hmm, in multi_step case, source x0 should be tm_fvar x; Hst : step (tm_fvar x) y. Since the induction generalizes the source, x0 = tm_fvar x. The goal v = tm_fvar x? Let's see: after `intros x v Hm`, goal `v = tm_fvar x`. induction Hm: case refl: z = tm_fvar x and v = z, goal z = tm_fvar x ✓. Case step: x0 = tm_fvar x, y, z=v; Hst : step (tm_fvar x) y; exfalso; exact (fvar_no_step x y Hst) ✓.

R_step:
```coq
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' HR Hst. destruct T as [| A B]; simpl in *.
  - destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]. split; [apply step_lc with t; assumption | split].
    + apply SN_step with t; assumption.
    + intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v).
      * apply multi_step with t'; assumption.
      * assumption.
  - destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hval]]. split; [apply step_lc with t; assumption | split].
    + apply SN_step with t; assumption.
    + intros v Hm Hv. apply (Hval v).
      * apply multi_step with t'; assumption.
      * assumption.
Qed.
```
Here `multi_step with t'` constructs multi step t v from step t t' and multi t' v. The constructor is `multi_step : forall x y z, R x y -> multi R y z -> multi R x z`. Lemma SN_step : forall t t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t -> strongly_normalizing t'.
